#114. Iran: Trump says strikes unlikely if peace talks keep moving

After weeks of rumors that the US and Israel were preparing new strikes on Iran, President Donald Trump said it was "highly unlikely" that he would order a new escalation as the Iranians "want to make a deal so badly."

In an interview with US broadcaster CNN, President Donald Trump sought to downplay fears that the US is about to relaunch its military campaign against Iran.

"It depends on whether or not they sign a very strong agreement," he told CNN on Sunday. "The answer is highly unlikely it's going to be necessary."

At the same time, Trump restated that the US military was "ready" to launch a new round of strikes and could "finish [Iran] off."

"I've got three aircraft carriers here," he said. "They're fully locked and loaded."

He added that the US military had 38 bridges in their sights "that we can knock down in literally one hour."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

However, Trump also repeated several times that the new escalation is "highly unlikely," saying that Iran was a "failing nation" and "withering away financially" after eight months of hostilities.

"I hit them so hard. You don't know how hard I hit them," he said. "They want to make a deal so badly."

Trump ordered an attack on Iran in late February, after US ally Israel launched what it called a "preemptive strike" on its biggest regional rival. The US president has since repeatedly declared victory, insinuated that Iran was begging the US for a deal, and told the media that a peace deal was done or nearly done.

Iran has repeatedly rejected Trump's account. Despite Trump's claims that Iran was collapsing both economically and militarily, Tehran has been able to repeatedly stage attacks on US allies in the region and maintains at least a partial blockade of the Strait of Hormuz. The Houthi rebels in Yemen, allied with Tehran, are currently targeting high-profile targets in the Saudi capital of Riyadh and elsewhere across Saudi Arabia.

The most tangible diplomatic achievement since the start of the war was reported in June, when Iran and the US signed a memorandum of understanding in Islamabad. But the talks broke down and the memorandum expired in August​​​​​​​, with the two sides still engaged in a military and diplomatic stalemate.

The conflict has proven to be unpopular with the US public, featuring heavily in the campaign for US midterm election that eventually saw Trump's Republicans lose their majorities in Congress.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

On Sunday, Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said that that peace mediators did not "travel" since the Tuesday election in the US. Araghchi described the outcome as a "shock."

"We now have to see how long it will take for them to digest these results, in order to determine how we are going to move forward," he said.

Qatar, which acts as one of the mediators in the conflict, denied rumors that the US and Iran are failing to reach a peace deal. On Saturday, the Qatari prime minister said both sides were still working toward an agreement.

Separately, the Reuters news agency cited a senior Iranian official as saying that Tehran has submitted a proposal on its nuclear program to Washington through Qatar.

The official also noted that further talks would depend on the US meeting Tehran's demands, including the lifting of sanctions, unfreezing Iranian funds and war reparations.

#113. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#112. This man refused life-saving treatment because of his religion - days later the rules changed

Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure and his condition deteriorated rapidly

In August this year, 26-year-old Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure while browsing in his local library, causing him to convulse and strike his head against the ground.

He lost consciousness. Later, in hospital, it transpired Isaac had hit his head with such force it had led to a brain haemorrhage.

Treatment was seriously complicated by the fact that, as a Jehovah's Witness, Isaac carried a "No Blood" card, an indication that he would not accept treatment involving any form of blood transfusion because of his religious beliefs.

As a result, a procedure to drain fluid from Isaac's brain did not take place.

But his family, from Waxahachie, Texas, were encouraged by the fact that he regained consciousness and even started to talk again. He was able to confirm that he stood by his "no blood" beliefs.

The family's relief at his apparent improvement was short-lived. Isaac slipped into a coma and his condition worsened rapidly.

A neurosurgeon told Isaac's family the only way to save his life was an emergency operation, but that it was one that required blood transfusion.

His family took the decision to abide by Issac's wishes as a lifelong Witness not to accept such a procedure.

Isaac - who his twin brother Jacob Valdez describes as an intelligent man who loved volunteering and helping the elderly - died on 26 August 2026 when doctors turned off his life support.

Jacob's "no blood" card which is like the one carried in Isaac's wallet

Just 23 days later, the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body announced a seismic change in teaching that would, in effect, have given Isaac the religious green light to have the life-saving treatment he had refused.

The change suddenly means the vast majority of medical procedures using donated blood are no longer prohibited to followers. Now, decisions on whether to accept such treatments are to be left to individuals.

Jacob, already dealing with the death of his twin, was angered at the Jehovah's Witnesses' leadership for not coming to their conclusion sooner. He told BBC News he believes Isaac could well still be alive if they had.

Jacob (right) believes his twin Isaac could still be alive if the change in teaching had come sooner

The original rule on blood transfusions dates back to 1945, towards the very end of a war during which the use of blood transfusions had been increasingly widespread. The leaders of Jehovah's Witnesses announced that their believers were not permitted to accept transfusions.

This was based on their interpretation of Bible verses from both the Old and New Testaments prohibiting the consumption of blood, including one from the Book of Acts that instructs believers to abstain from blood, from food offered to idols, and from things that had been strangled.

So, why has the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body, based in New York state, now decided to change the rules, 81 years after introducing them?

It comes after years of pressure from both within and outside the faith, but that is not how leaders of the faith describe the decision.

"The change is basically the application of Bible principles to modern medicine and its methods," says the Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson, Paul Gillies, who described it as both a fresh look at scripture and at medical advances.

Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson Paul Gillies says the ban on transfusion of whole blood remains but blood components can now be used

We meet Gillies at the Jehovah's Witnesses UK headquarters set in 85 acres of Essex countryside. It is a vast modern complex where hundreds of volunteer followers of the faith live and work without pay.

He insists the basic teaching has not changed and that the transfusion of whole blood is still prohibited. However, for decades, most transfusions have not used whole blood, instead using the specific components of blood that are needed by the patient.

The change is being justified by permitting the use of blood components, like plasma and red blood cells and platelets, in the huge numbers of medical procedures that were previously banned.

Gillies says the Bible references "abstaining from blood" but not "various components that could be extracted from blood".

He says the Governing Body felt it their duty to avoid scriptural "overreach" and only apply what was specifically described in the Bible.

But that explanation has stunned many and led them to ask if it was not a huge overreach 81 years ago to ban blood transfusions in the first place, when transfusions are not specifically mentioned in the bible.

Others are then also asking why it has taken so long to come to this landmark decision, given that transfusions that do not use whole blood have been the norm for decades.

These are questions Jacob asked as soon as the ban was lifted, just days after a memorial service for Isaac.

Jacob, who had already left the Jehovah's Witness faith he was brought up in, texted prominent figures from Isaac's congregation in Texas after Isaac's death. The messages are full of angst, and painful to read.

He asked them to consider "the part you played in encouraging so many people to sacrifice their life for something that was never God's will".

Most did not reply, but one "elder" responded saying he could not understand why Jacob was so angry and fixated on the policy shift.

This all further infuriated Jacob, who particularly wants the faith's decision-makers to be accountable for all the lives lost.

"I want to ask them if they could apologise directly, I would like not to have a deflected answer, just straightforward apology," he says.

It is extremely difficult to say how many Jehovah's Witnesses, among the estimated nine million followers of the faith worldwide, died each year having refused treatment involving blood transfusions. But research in one medical journal in 2016, The Obstetrical & Gynecological Survey, put the figure at around 1,100.

"We're sympathetic to their feelings, and if individuals have lost family members and are concerned about some of these questions, they deserve to be heard compassionately," says Gillies.

But there was little clarity about why the change did not come earlier.

"Why has it taken this time? Well, I guess because it takes time, it doesn't necessarily mean that there is some magic change that's taken place," says Gillies.

And when asked, Gillies deflects calls for the leadership to apologise, saying that many lives had, in fact, been saved because faithful Jehovah's Witnesses had not been exposed to blood contamination scandals.

He also insists that even after the new edict, some Jehovah's Witnesses could still decide they did not want to accept a transfusion, and that may have been the case even if the edict had come years ago.

Indeed, the BBC spoke with Alex Ball, 58, a Jehovah's Witness from West Sussex, who says in 2012 a doctor told him he would not survive the chemotherapy he needed to treat his blood cancer unless he also had a transfusion.

"I wanted to adhere as closely to the scriptures as possible when I was sick, and I think I did that well."

With help from what is called "a Jehovah's Witness Hospital Liaison Committee", Alex found an American consultant who could perform a treatment to boost Alex's blood cells without the need for any products from someone else. His UK consultant agreed to treat him using the new method and it worked.

More than a decade later, Alex says he would make the same decision again, despite the change in blood policy that would have permitted the transfusions he had been told he needed.

It is not just receiving blood which has been an issue.

Elisha Cummings, 50, a Jehovah's Witness from Trinidad and Tobago, was unable to donate blood to her aunt, who died, because of the rules.

Elisha Cummings was advised by an elder not to donate blood to her aunt Eva who died

They also cost her her marriage too, with her husband of 10 years leaving the faith and divorcing her after it came to light that he had had a blood transfusion as a child, something that was looked down upon in her Witness community.

"Since the blood policy change, I have not been myself," she says. "It left me in a sort of depressed mode."

"How many houses did I go to trying to convince people about the blood doctrine? And you're coming now and it's something totally different," Elisha says.

Elisha says her beliefs as a whole have been shaken, yet she feels reluctant to leave the faith, partly because it is what provides her with her community and she fears isolation.

In the past, those who disagreed with "blood doctrine" were ostracised or felt like they needed to leave the faith. Will they now be able to come back?

"Any individual that wants to come back to the faith is warmly welcomed," says Gillies, speaking of those who had left the faith because they had accepted a transfusion for themselves or a relative.

"If they would like to become one of Jehovah's Witnesses again and would like to speak to the elders, then they're very welcome to do so."

The pressure on the Church of England to ditch its slavery reparations plan

#110. Lisa Nandy wary of ‘circular arguments’ about rejoining EU

Culture secretary pushes back against talk of revisiting Brexit but says closer ties would fulfil referendum promises

One of Andy Burnham’s cabinet ministers has said she wants to avoid “circular arguments” about whether the UK should rejoin the EU, underlining divisions at the top of government over revisiting Brexit.

Lisa Nandy, the culture secretary, appeared to suggest she was not in favour of rejoining the trade bloc, but she said the prime minister should still pursue closer ties to fulfil the promises made at the time of the referendum.

She is one of a handful of cabinet ministers who are concerned about Burnham’s plan for an “honest conversation” with the public about Brexit and who are privately warning it could reopen a row that would only benefit Nigel Farage’s Reform UK.

“I do not believe that anybody wants to repeat circular arguments about in or out, leave or remain. I do think, though, that over the last decade we have let the British people down very badly,” she told the BBC’s Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme.

Nandy, whose Wigan seat voted overwhelmingly for leave in 2016, and who is one of the most cautious in the cabinet about reversing Brexit, was asked if she wanted to go back in. “I don’t know if that’s a choice we have,” she said.

However, she did concede that Burnham was trying to address the failings of the Brexit deal, telling Sky News it was important to “be honest” and highlight that “the deal that we got is not what we were promised”.

The culture secretary stressed there were “trade-offs and choices” to be made which involved a conversation with people across the country, indicating that Brexit would be a key theme of the next election campaign.

Burnham will attend the European Political Community summit in Dublin on Thursday. Downing Street sources said he would use it to get Labour’s reset deal over the line and to meet many European leaders for the first time.

In private, EU counterparts will be expecting Burnham to set out his ambitions for further integration after he announced his intention to move beyond Labour’s “red lines” on the bloc after the next election.

At a Brexit reset meeting on 20 November in Brussels, agreements on agriculture and food, emissions trading and youth experience visas are expected to be confirmed.

Burnham said at the weekend there was more to come. “This summit will show what we can achieve when we work together, but it doesn’t need to be the end of our ambition,” he told the Sunday Mirror.

Officials will also discuss the agenda for the next round of the reset, which could include another attempt for the UK to join the EU’s €150bn (£125bn) Safe defence fund, collaboration on law enforcement and borders, and visas for touring artists.

Soon after the Brussels summit, Burnham is expected to give a speech setting out options for a closer relationship with the EU, arguing it would be in the UK’s long-term interest. No 10 sources said he wanted to “frame the conversation, not go straight to the endgame” before testing public opinion.

Senior government figures say Burnham wants a mandate to start negotiations with the EU in Labour’s next election manifesto, but that any subsequent referendum would be solely confirmatory.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

“You can’t ask people to vote on an undefined plan like in 2016. That was David Cameron’s mistake,” one said.

However, Burnham has faced reservations from others within his cabinet. The business secretary, Jonathan Reynolds, and home secretary, Shabana Mahmood, are cautious about pushing to fully rejoin.

The EU’s former chief Brexit negotiator Michel Barnier said London could not have the “single market à la carte” by cherrypicking which of the four freedoms – including freedom of movement – it would like in future negotiations.

“There is no cherrypicking. There will be no possibility to have one freedom and not the other,” he told the BBC.

Nandy was forced to reject allegations that celebrating the UK rejoining the Erasmus plus scheme by linking it to Remembrance Sunday was inappropriate, after suggesting it would help to honour “the immense courage of those who served our country” before Remembrance Sunday.

Kevin Hollinrake, the Tory chair, suggested she was using the national civic occasion to “try to make an EU membership pitch”. He said: “Our veterans did not fight for a student exchange scheme.”

Speaking on Sky News, Nandy responded: “They are the only party that is talking about Brexit and the EU. What we are announcing this weekend is that we’re going to widen the Erasmus scheme.

“I cannot see what is political about saying that we want young people from every part of this country to understand the contribution that their grandparents and great-grandparents made, not just to us but to many, many lives of people that they had never met,” she added.

#109. Germans wrestle with what to do with Hitler's destroyed Eagle's Nest retreat

Fire engulfed Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat in Bavaria last month

Perched on a ridge high up in the spectacular scenery of the German Alps lie the burned-out ruins of a mountain house called The Eagle's Nest, which is now at the heart of an intense debate about Germany's Nazi past.

Known in German as Kehlsteinhaus, the retreat was built by the Nazis in 1938, and frequented by Adolf Hitler and other high-ranking Nazis and their guests. After World War Two it was classified as a historical monument recalling the Nazi era.

Last month it was gutted by fire in a suspected arson attack, and some believe it should be completely destroyed, the site returned to nature and all memory of its Nazi past forgotten.

But others want it either partially or completely rebuilt, wary of the risk of turning it into a site for Neo-Nazi pilgrimage at a time when Germany is witnessing the resurgence of the far-right.

Germany has an "everlasting responsibility to communicate, to deal with our Nazi past", said Isabel Heinemann, professor of contemporary history at Munich's Ludwig-Maximilians-UniversitÃ¤t (LMU).

But rebuilding the site completely, she warned, would mean putting "highly ideologised or highly ideological architecture" back in place.

Adolf Hitler used the mountain retreat to welcome guests

Before it was burned down during the evening of 23 October, the Eagle's Nest had been a popular tourist attraction, owned by the state of Bavaria and operating for many years as a restaurant.

Visitors would take a bus and then ride the original brass and mirror-panelled lift through the mountain to the Kehlsteinhaus to savour the magnificent views above the town of Berchtesgaden.

Once there, they could eat sausages in the beer garden on the Obersalzberg at 1,834m (6,017ft) above sea level, overlooking the Alps.

Unlike Hitler's nearby summer residence, the Berghof, which was bombed in World War Two and demolished in 1952, the Eagle's Nest survived the war.

Tens of thousands of tourists visited each season, providing an important source of income to the region.

In the 1990s, amid concerns that the site did not do enough to confront the crimes of the Nazi past, a museum, the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, was opened close by.

Before it was burned down, the Kehlsteinhaus or Eagle's Nest had long been a tourist destination in Bavaria

Around 15 years ago, a photo exhibit was opened in the Kehlsteinhaus about its history.

Although the Eagle's Nest itself was "largely destroyed" by the fire, according to police, the documentation centre survived unscathed.

The authorities have not commented on a possible motive, but a 33-year-old man with a history of mental illness has been detained in a secure psychiatric hospital on suspicion of arson.

Man arrested after fire at Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat

Some feel this is the moment to allow the site to fade into history.

"It's been torched now. It's been destroyed. I think they should just clear away the rubble and leave the mountain as it should be in its natural state," said Prof Sir Richard J. Evans, a specialist in Nazi Germany at the University of Cambridge.

That has happened with "quite a few other Nazi sites", he argued, including the bunker in Berlin where Hitler killed himself: "The bunker was filled in with concrete and a children's playground put on the top of it on the ground."

The Kehlsteinhaus was just "a Nazi vanity project" since romanticised by the name Eagle's Nest, he said.

Police say the old Nazi retreat was largely destroyed by last month's fire

But Bavaria's authorities take a different view about the site's future.

Both the state's interior minister, Joachim Herrmann, and its minister for the arts, Markus Blume, have spoken out in favour of rebuilding the Kehlsteinhaus.

"We can hardly leave a ruin standing there," Herrmann said.

Sven Keller, head of the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, believes it is important not to leave the site in ruins, as that could encourage Neo-Nazi pilgrims, who would be able to hike up there completely undisturbed.

"We have quite some experience at Obersalzberg with what happens if we clear those sites. Hitler's Berghof was demolished in the 1950s, and in our experience, this does not remove such sites from the mental maps of people," he told the BBC's Today programme.

Nor does he believe "a one-on-one reconstruction" of the site is possible, and Blume has made clear on Bavarian radio that "it should not and must not cost the taxpayer anything".

After Hitler's downfall, US soldiers captured the mountain-top retreat and are seen relaxing here on 4 May 1945

Herrmann said they would look for a sensible solution in consultation with historians, given the historically complex circumstances surrounding the building.

Heinemann, who also heads the Leibniz Institute for Contemporary History, believes full-scale reconstruction should be "out of the question".

"I rather think we can document the state left by the fire and also document the historical remains of the Kehlsteinhaus, and then reconstruct parts in a modern design, to make clear the different layers of time that can be seen in this building," she told the BBC.

Germany and Austria have long struggled to deal with the fraught legacy of buildings and sites connected to the Nazi era.

With the recent rise of the far-right AfD and the Austrian Freedom Party, the debate over Erinnerungskultur (Culture of Remembrance) has taken on a new urgency.

A row broke out recently in Germany when AfD and conservative CDU councillors in a town in Saxony decided to ban "stumbling stones" memorials - embedded small brass plaques in the streets - to victims of the Nazis.

In Austria over the summer, a police station was opened in the house where Hitler was born in Braunau am Inn. It followed years of controversy about how the house should be used.

The Austrian government said the police presence would help prevent neo-Nazi pilgrims.

But others had wanted the house to become a centre of responsibility, actively confronting the Nazi past.

As one resident of Braunau am Inn told me, it was a delicate balance between how much to remember and how much to forget.

Police station opens at Hitler's birthplace in bid to rid site of Nazi link

#105. The Trump coalition is shattered - what will Republicans do now?

Republicans are in soul-searching mode after the coalition that powered Donald Trump to three straight presidential nominations imploded in spectacular fashion in the 2026 midterms.

The stunning election defeat immediately raised questions about the party's future post-Trump.

But there are no easy answers as Republicans face the choice of doubling down on Trumpism or trying to chart a new path forward in his final years in office.

The depth of the problem was captured by outgoing Florida Governor Ron DeSantis, who called the midterms "close to an extinction-level event for Republicans".

Others struck a more optimistic tone. Vice-President JD Vance, who blamed the midterm results on public anger over the war with Iran, argued the party could turn things around. Nevertheless Vance acknowledged the fallout will be messy.

"There will be a lot of infighting and arguing over what happened and why," Vance said.

The wide range of reactions on the right highlights deep fault lines in the party that formed around Trump's improbable rise to the presidency in 2016 and never really went away.

It's too early to tell which faction will win. But while Republicans are still licking their wounds, the intraparty battle Vance predicted is already under way.

The party must decide whether it will be "centred on one figure, one guy, or on a wider set of ideas like conservatism that can win outside the deepest red places," Rina Shah, a Republican strategist, told the BBC.

Critics on the right - the "Never Trumpers" who opposed his first White House campaign - are pushing for a return to the Republican Party's pre-Trump roots, citing the historic losses around the country on election night.

But simply turning back the clock on Trump will be extremely difficult if not impossible to do.

The party has been wholly transformed during Trump's decade-long reign. Most Republicans have shifted to the right on domestic issues like immigration, and rejected the interventionist foreign policy of the George W Bush era that led to the wars in Iraq and Afghanistan.

In Congress, the more moderate establishment wing of the party that disliked Trump from the start has almost entirely disappeared, replaced by conservative Trump backers who echo his nativism and populist rhetoric.

That trend continued this year, as old-guard Republicans like John Cornyn in Texas and Maine's Susan Collins, both of whom clashed with Trump over the years, were voted out of office.

Three days after the election Trump appointed Stephen Miller as his new White House chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

The move signalled Trump has no plans to moderate his policies or message in his remaining years in office. Miller, who had been serving as the president's chief domestic policy adviser, is a longtime Trump aide known for his hardline immigration policies.

With Stephen Miller pick, Trump readies for a battle

One of the architects of the Make America Great Again (Maga) movement, Steve Bannon, predicted a "civil war" in the party after the midterms.

Afterwards, he blamed the Republicans' defeat on the economy and "foreign wars". "I don't think we implemented the Maga programme," he said, calling on Trump to double down on mass deportations.

But other Republican strategists said the midterms were a wake up call for Make America Great Again (Maga) loyalists who want Trump's political movement to outlast his presidency.

"There's still plenty of Maga voters out there who have supported Donald Trump through thick and thin," said Whit Ayres, a Republican strategist.

"But there is also a significant portion of the Republican Party that's non-Maga, and that's a majority now."

The party lost control of the House and Senate, as well as governorships and state legislatures in red states like Texas and Kansas where Democrats had been shut out of power for decades - and where Trump won by double digits when he was on the ballot.

The stunning upsets underscored the fickle nature of political coalitions.

Trump expanded the Republican base in 2024, increasing support with Latinos and younger voters among other key groups. Republicans hailed the gains as a sign of future strength for the party.

Just two years later, the party lost ground with nearly every critical voting bloc. According to a New York Times analysis of voter data, Latino support for Democratic candidates increased by 29 percentage points from 2024-26. Support from young voters 18-29 years old increased by 33 points.

The swings reflected polling ahead of the midterms that found widespread disapproval with Trump's handling of the economy, immigration and the war with Iran.

Tuesday night saw Trump's Republicans lose in states they had comfortably held for decades

Democrats also capitalised on a public perception that the Trump family and administration is rife with self-dealing and corruption. The president's focus on the White House ballroom and other Washington construction projects also contributed to a sense among voters that he was more focused on his legacy than addressing affordability issues.

It's a sentiment shared by Benjamin Shrader, the leader of the Young Republicans in Austin, who was still shell-shocked when he spoke to the BBC in Texas the morning after his party was almost wiped out in the state by a Democratic "blue tsunami".

Shrader said Trump was still a "force of nature" when asked to reflect on how the president had used his first public appearance after the results to show off the White House ballroom project.

He hesitated before adding: "I would focus on issues that are not ballrooms, that are not renaming things. I would focus on what matters."

The evidence in the immediate aftermath of the midterms suggests that Republicans could benefit by distancing themselves from Trump ahead of 2028.

With Congress flipped, what does Donald Trump do next?

Vance tried to thread the needle with his midterm assessment blaming the Iran war and calling for a return to "America First." It was a nuanced critique that broke with Trump on an unpopular war, but embraced a broader political brand that remains popular with the Maga base.

Trump's next moves on Iran will be watched closely by the party for their 2028 implications. An escalation would risk fuelling the concerns voiced by Vance and other "America First" proponents.

In his postmortem statement on X, Vance also echoed the growing calls on the right to reexamine US support for Israel. But he also signalled an openness to making some immigration enforcement changes. That clashes with Miller, a Vance ally who has speared the administration's second-term immigration agenda.

Party strategists said the midterm drubbing complicated Vance's path to the nomination.

"Before this midterm, JD Vance was the odds-on favourite to win the Republican nomination in 2028. After this midterm, the nomination battle is completely wide open," Ayres said.

Potential Trump successors have some time to consider their options, but they'll have to make up their minds relatively soon. Any 2028 presidential hopefuls will face pressure to announce White House bids in the next few months. Some could launch campaigns before the end of the year.

Vance's election analysis was widely interpreted as an unofficial signal of his plans to run. It will likely be a crowded primary field.

Both Vice-President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio are seen as contenders for the 2028 nomination

Other rumoured White House contenders include Secretary of State Marco Rubio, Senator Ted Cruz of Texas, Florida Governor DeSantis, former US ambassador to the United Nations Nikki Haley and former New Jersey Governor Chris Christie, to name a few.

In the next election cycle, Republican presidential and congressional candidates alike will have tough decisions to make on key issues of the day, from US support for Israel to tariffs to the AI boom.

Trump's power may be diminished as he enters the final two years of his second term, but he'll still have the loudest voice in the party over the next two years. It's unclear whether he will get deeply involved in the process or take a hands-off approach.

Trump has praised Vance and Rubio at different times, but typically deflects questions about who he'd like to see succeed him.

His endorsement will loom as the biggest wildcard in the 2028 Republican primary race.

"It remains to be seen who will jump into that race," Ayres said.

"But I think it's going to be a lot larger and more diverse than we anticipated before the midterm election."

'Hopefully things get done': Americans react to historic political shift

#104. Two police officers hailed as heroes for confronting Kraków gunman

Jakub Kulik critically injured and Artur Morański also wounded after actions that ‘prevented further casualties’

Two Polish police officers who were injured as they pursued and detained the gunman in Kraków on Saturday have been hailed as heroes.

One of them is Jakub Kulik, a former paratrooper and volunteer firefighter, who was left critically injured after a close-range exchange of fire with the suspect, a 24-year-old Polish national who killed six and injured 17 in an attack believed to have been inspired by far-right ideologies.

The other injured police officer is Artur Morański. Both are under 30 years old, the Polish news network TVP World reported.

Poland’s prime minister, Donald Tusk, said on Saturday in a post on X: “They probably saved many human lives, disregarding the risk to their own.”

The Polish justice minister, Waldemar Żurek, said the rapid response and heroism of the two police officers “prevented further human casualties”.

Police arrived at the scene 23 seconds after receiving the first emergency call from Kraków’s main square, where the suspect was shooting at random from his car.

Footage that emerged on Sunday showed two police officers exchanging fire with the gunman. One of the officers then apprehended the suspect on the ground before firing his own weapon several times. The officer then fell down, apparently injured himself.

Michał Pędziwiatr and Mateusz Wierdak, two local surgeons who were having a bite to eat nearby, stepped in to help.

Wierdak “took care of the officer who subdued the attacker because he truly needed urgent help”, Pędziwiatr told the Polish news website onet.

“Without protection, without gloves, he simply focused on the officer, stopping the bleeding in his chest. This probably saved his life.”

Kulik, from the southern Polish city of Bielsko-Biala, was hit by three bullets and underwent surgery on Saturday. There has been a national appeal for blood donors.

Morański was shot in the chest area and also underwent surgery. Both men were brought out of medically induced comas on Sunday, Polskie Radio 24 reported.

Kulik is also a member of the volunteer fire service in Bielsko-Biala. “We’re very proud of him,” a volunteer firefighter from the city’s Leszczyny district told the local news website Bielsko.biala.pl. “He responds to emergencies just like we do.”

TVP World reported that Morański was from Powroźnik, a village about 90 miles (150km) south-east of Krakow and close to the border with Slovakia. A mass was held for him at the local church on Sunday.

#103. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#98. Cop31: what is at stake at the UN climate summit and what can it achieve?

Delegates from more than 190 countries are gathering in Turkey for climate talks, but the US will be absent

As the planet faces unprecedented human-caused global heating, heads of government are meeting for the next fortnight for the latest round of climate talks. We ask why it matters and what is likely to be achieved.

From 9-20 November, world leaders, ministers and high-ranking officials from more than 190 countries are expected to convene in Antalya in Turkey, for the annual UN climate talks. This year, the summit will be hosted by Turkey, but in an unusual twist the negotiations will be presided over by Australia in a compromise deal struck after the two countries vied for the presidency.

While nearly all of the 196 original signatories of the 2015 Paris climate agreement are expected to be present, one glaring absence will be the US, whose president, Donald Trump, has withdrawn from Paris.

Australia will take charge of the formal negotiations where countries thrash out the details of work programmes such as the “just transition”, climate finance and putting the world on track to stick as closely as possible to the Paris agreement goal of limiting temperature rises to 1.5C above pre-industrial levels.

Turkey will take charge of the “action agenda”, the voluntary agreements that work towards the Paris goals but are not legally binding: programmes on cutting waste and achieving a circular economy, cutting methane, improving food security, and – perhaps most important – the electrification of the global economy.

The Paris goal of 1.5C has been breached, but the UN argues that rapid action to cut greenhouse gas emissions would limit the “overshoot” and allow for a return to lower temperatures by the end of this century. For that to happen, countries need to break dependence on fossil fuels and adopt low-carbon technologies faster. The two roadmaps from Brazil – on the “transition away from fossil fuels” and on halting deforestation – will present ways of doing so.

Developing countries are also in dire need of help to cope with the hastening impacts of the climate crisis. After the disaster in Nepal, the country has been granted access to the global fund for “loss and damage” – the rescue and rehabilitation of communities stricken by climate-related disasters. But it is not enough, and poor countries also lack the means to adapt their infrastructure to worsening extreme weather.

Rich countries have been cutting their overseas aid, while rising interest rates have loaded further debt on to the poor.

It was not until 2023 at Cop28 that the final agreement directly addressed fossil fuels for the first time, due to long-term strident opposition from producing countries. The agreement included a resolution to “transition away from fossil fuels” but no deadline or plan for doing so. It is still tricky to get fossil fuels directly on to the Cop agenda, which requires consensus from nearly all parties, but this year the issue will be addressed through a non-binding “roadmap” for the global transition away from fossil fuels.

Given the tense and fragile geopolitical situation – some commentators believe we are already in the midst of an unacknowledged third world war, while inflation and a rising far right are upsetting economies – countries might be tempted to settle for a quiet meeting.

But there are hopes for a better outcome, which could include: acceptance of the plans Brazil has produced and a promise to carry them forward; progress on climate finance for developing countries; a resolution to the row over climate science that has plagued recent meetings of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change; and the fleshing out of the “just transition mechanism” designed to help workers reliant on fossil fuels to adjust to a low-carbon economy.

Progress is also possible through Cop-related means outside the formal negotiations. Earlier this year, more than 60 countries, including the UK, the EU and dozens of developing nations, gathered in Colombia to vow to “transition away from fossil fuels”. This “coalition of the willing” could be boosted at Cop31 by new members, as well as fresh impetus from the recent success of political movements in several countries – Brazil, Canada, Serbia, Hungary and the US in states that decisively rejected Donald Trump at the midterms.

Several prominent members of the US Congress may attend, along with businesspeople and US civil society groups, so there will be representation beyond the White House’s absence. Trump appears intent on boosting fossil fuel interests as much as possible in the US, but with oil prices still high after his war in Iran, the economics of low-carbon energy look ever more favourable.

The geopolitics and the technology. Although the winds of conflict have upended international relations, they have also shown countries can benefit from the energy security that results from decentralised renewable generation. China’s oil imports, for instance, are likely to be down about 12% this year owing to a massive uptake of electric vehicles, and its renewable generation capacity has now outstripped coal. This is saving billions of dollars for consumers and businesses as the oil price soars.

Solar is booming in Africa at last, while in Pakistan a consumer-led rooftop revolution now produces about a quarter of the electricity used. Close to half of new cars in countries from Vietnam to some EU members are now electric.

Electrification will be a key focus of Cop31, and the high efficiency of electric technology means it could reduce the amount of primary energy use by half across the world, according to University of Oxford professor Jan Rosenow.

Politically, the re-election of Lula as president in Brazil has also cheered climate supporters. Lula will attend Cop, his first international visit since re-election, and is likely to be a fiery presence, showing progressive parties around the world how to win while championing the climate.

#96. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#94. Who’s who at Cop31: key players at the climate talks

What are the relationships and dominant interests in play, as the summit kicks off after a bitter row over its hosting?

Hosting Cop was a key pledge from Australia’s prime minister, Anthony Albanese in 2021, while in opposition, before his successful election campaign. But arcane rules meant the country had to wait its turn to bid and was then embroiled in an angry row with Turkey over the hosting. The final compromise put Australia in charge of the negotiations which will take place in the Turkish city of Antalya. The bitter taste left by the row lingers and Australia has seemed to some a rather half-hearted host. The country’s media has been much quieter on Cop than previous host countries, and although Albanese will speak at the opening of the leaders’ segment, he is unlikely to stay long.

Chris Bowen, Australia’s climate and energy minister, will be in daily charge of the negotiations. As aspects of Australia’s energy supply also fall under his portfolio, he has been in the sometimes uncomfortable position of having to boost fossil fuels while insisting on the need for a clean energy transition.

Yet Australia has a positive story to tell on decarbonisation: while remaining one of the world’s major fossil fuel exporters, the country has also invested heavily in renewables, and solar power has now expanded so much that consumers are sometimes offered free electricity.

Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Turkey’s autocratic president, showed little interest in climate and environmental concerns before bidding to host this year’s Cop summit.

In the last five years, Cop has also been held in Egypt, the United Arab Emirates, Azerbaijan, all in the same broad region. This has given an outsize role to the Arab group of countries, who are neighbours in the region and have a strong interest in ensuring that fossil fuel interests – including their lobbyists – are well represented.

Australia runs the overall direction of the negotiations, and Turkey’s government, with the environment minister, Murat Kurum, has control of the action agenda, a programme of voluntary initiatives. Erdoğan has used this to push forward a key interest of his wife, Emine Erdoğan, in cutting waste and promoting the circular economy. She has called for an end to “throwaway culture” and for recycling instead of landfill – though Turkey still has sites that leak vast quantities of methane.

Fatih Birol, the head of the International Energy Agency and one of the world’s leading energy economists, is understood to be one of the advisers behind this year’s emphasis on electrification, displacing fossil fuels with electricity for heating, transport and industrial uses. This could halve the amount of energy we need, and is the next biggest step, after boosting renewable energy generation, needed for a low-carbon world.

The recently re-elected President Lula da Silva will come to Cop31 for his first trip abroad after winning his historic fourth term, and will bring two roadmaps, one a global plan for the transition away from fossil fuels, the other for halting and reversing deforestation. These are non-binding, and contain no deadlines or firm requirements from governments, but show a series of steps that governments can take to achieve these goals.

Xi Jinping, the president of China, will not come to this year’s Cop, but his country’s rapid energy and industrial transformation will determine the course of the planet’s future. China is the world’s second biggest economy and the biggest emitter of greenhouse gases, making up nearly a third of global annual carbon output, owing mainly to the use of fossil fuels to power its explosive economic growth for the last 25 years. But it has also invested in renewable energy, which is reshaping the global energy business.

Xi may not be present in Antalya, but the top officials he sends will know that petrostates – including Russia, Saudi Arabia and the UAE – are his important allies. However, countries pushing for a low-carbon future may be more important still – as China’s customers.

For several months this year, India’s prime minister, Narendra Modi, mulled an intriguing proposition: to host the Cop33 UN climate summit in 2028. In the end, India withdrew its bid in April without specifying why, leaving the field open. India is torn between its burgeoning clean energy sector – it now has the world’s third biggest installed capacity of renewable energy, after China and the US – and its coal sector, which still supplies about 70% of electricity, and has close links to Modi.

Bhupender Yadav, India’s environment minister, who leads its Cop delegations, enjoys a mutually supportive relationship with the UK’s Ed Miliband, and a reputation as a forward thinker.

The attention of Donald Trump has been fixed on the crucial midterm elections, a verdict on his second term as president that has shown Republicans the electoral dangers of an unpredictable White House. No US delegation is expected at Cop31, and soon Trump’s withdrawal of the country from the UN framework convention on climate change will mean none can take part.

Trump’s shadow was felt at Cop30 in Belem last year, as he started to dismantle the US’s renewable energy sector and boost fossil fuels, setting the US on course to start increasing greenhouse gas emissions again, after decades in which they fell. But his administration’s unpopularity with the US electorate, and a drought and fire-filled summer, has bolstered the pro-climate stance of scores of Democratic congresspeople and state governors, despite a campaign of “climate hushing” before the midterms. Some of them, and a scattering of business leaders, may attend Cop31.

Andy Burnham will attend Cop for the first time this year, after a mixed record on the climate since taking office. During the record-breaking heatwaves and droughts of this summer, the British prime minister was criticised for failing to speak out on the climate crisis. He subsequently made strong interventions affirming his support for the UK’s target of reaching net zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050, but then backed the Jackdaw gasfield. No decision has been announced on the Rosebank oilfield yet.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

From December, Burnham will have a much bigger opportunity to craft climate policy on the international stage: the UK will take over the revolving presidency of the influential G20 group, which comprises the world’s biggest developed and developing economies. The Guardian understands that the UK will emphasise building resilience against the impacts of the climate crisis as a key focus of the G20 for its year-long presidency.

Miliband, now foreign secretary, and a veteran of many Cops in government and opposition, is expected to accompany the prime minister to the leaders’ summit segment of Cop31. Miliband was hailed, in public and private, by many participants last year for his patient bridge-building that helped Brazil to rescue a deal from the fractious final hours of Cop30.

At this year’s UN general assembly, he also briefed other governments on the findings of the UK’s joint intelligence committee, which warned that the impact of collapsing ecosystems around the world posed a national security risk, threatening food shortages, mass migration and potential nuclear war.

The energy and net zero secretary, Miatta Fahnbulleh, will head the UK’s Cop31 delegation for the final negotiations in the last days of this fortnight of talks, after the leaders have departed.

The EU has faced a quandary over its climate convictions in recent years. Far-right populists are on the rise, pushing climate denialism, and the bloc has backed down from some key regulations, watering down its green deal package, loosening the strings on corporate climate liabilities, delaying rules on trade in goods from deforested areas, and extending the phase-in of new car rules. The failure of Europe’s car industry to move decisively to electric vehicles – preferring lobbying against emissions standards, which has turned out to be a poor strategy as consumers turn in droves to cheap-to-run EVs – has led to threats to slap tariffs on China.

The EU commission president, Ursula von der Leyen called this summer’s record-breaking heatwaves and drought a “summer of truth” that must be answered: she is now confirmed for the summit. Wopke Hoekstra, the Dutch climate commissioner, will oversee the final stages of the talks for the EU, and is expected to make competition with China a key focus.

Cop31 will mark the final outing at a UN climate summit for António Guterres, the UN secretary general, who has regularly intervened powerfully on this subject. A successor to Guterres has still not yet been chosen – the US has tried to get Viktor Orbán, the far-right former president of Hungary, into the role, or a former minister from the United Arab Emirates, Lana Nusseibeh.

Simon Stiell, the determined executive secretary of the UNFCCC, will oversee the talks, with an added twist to his job this year, having to liaise between the two co-hosts as well as the fractious parties.

Saudi failed to confirm with the Guardian before Cop who would lead its delegation, but the country’s recent behaviour at international climate meetings tells its own story. The country has a long history of obstructing the UN climate negotiations, though its behaviour was sometimes muted when the US intervened. With Trump in the White House, Saudi has felt emboldened to interfere further, with allies including Russia, Venezuela, members of the Arab group, and the Like-Minded Developing Countries grouping.

At Cop30, the Saudi delegation refused to allow even an oblique mention of the pledge to “transition away from fossil fuels”, made at Cop28 in Dubai in 2023, in the final text until the final moments.

At recent meetings of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, Saudi led a small group of countries – including China, India, Russia and Kenya – that fought to delay publication of the next comprehensive assessment of climate science. Publication of the landmark report, which is expected to warn of catastrophic impacts from further temperature rises, was expected in 2028, in time for Cop33 at which countries will assess their progress on meeting emissions-cutting goals under the Paris agreement, a process known as the “global stocktake”. Delaying it by a year or more, as Saudi wants, would mean a loss of urgency for the global stocktake, and could help countries to get away with inadequate measures on decarbonisation.

#91. Cop31 must deliver climate progress that people can see is real, host says

High-stakes talks involving 190 countries begin on Monday as world grapples with El Niño supercharged by fossil fuels

The Cop31 summit must produce progress, “a tangible outcome ... that people can see is real”, its host has said as scores of world leaders and representatives of close to 190 countries gather in Turkey for the UN climate talks.

The high-stakes meeting begins on Monday, as countries around the world grapple with the strongest El Niño weather system on record, supercharged by fossil fuels, which is set to take hundreds of thousands of lives and cause damage costing trillions of dollars.

Friction is expected between the rich and poor worlds over who pays for the impacts of the climate crisis, and the slow pace of cutting greenhouse gas emissions. Temperatures have broken records this year, surpassing the limit of 1.5C above preindustrial levels aimed for in the 2015 Paris climate agreement.

Adão Soares Barbosa, the chair of the Least Developed Countries, a negotiating group of 44 nations, said: “Keeping 1.5C within reach by minimising the duration and magnitude of the overshoot, delivering accessible and predictable climate finance, strengthening adaptation, and addressing loss and damage [caused to poor countries by extreme weather] are not political choices but matters of survival. The credibility of the Paris agreement will be measured not by promises made, but by commitments fulfilled, and lives protected in vulnerable countries.”

Unusually, the talks will have dual hosts: they will take place in the Turkish city of Antalya but the formal negotiations will be presided over by Australia, following a compromise reached last year over their competing bids.

Chris Bowen, Australia’s climate and energy minister and president of negotiations for Cop31, told the Guardian in an interview: “We need a tangible outcome focused on implementation [of emissions cuts and finance] that people can see is real. We need progress, it needs to be a step forward on previous Cops.”

Australia also wants a “strong, ambitious outcome” to raise the plight of low-lying countries in the Pacific region for whom the climate crisis poses an existential threat. “We need to finally elevate some of the issues so important to our Pacific family,” Bowen said. “[Only] 0.2% of the world’s available climate finance goes to small island developing states for adaptation, for countries whose very existence is at stake.”

But climate advocates and more than 500 scientists in Australia said Australia must stop approving coalmines and gas projects if it wanted to escape the charge of hypocrisy.

The summit will have a notable absentee, with the US refusing to send a delegation for the second year in a row. The Trump administration wants no part of “extremist climate policies” embraced by other countries, according to a White House spokesperson.

Donald Trump expressed glee shortly before the US midterm elections about the huge profits made by oil and gas companies due to the Iran war, which he started and has caused an increase in living costs around the world. “I don’t give a damn,” the president said at a recent rally in Georgia. “I’m going to say it: we’re making a frickin’ fortune.”

However, a group of 20 Democratic members of Congress have made the trip to Antalya, buoyed by their party’s stunning success in the US midterms last week that will enable them to stymie Trump’s attempts to dismantle pollution rules and withhold financial support for clean energy.

Developing countries and civil society will seek to put climate finance and justice at the heart of the Cop31 talks. Rukiya Khamis, the east Africa programme manager at the 350.org campaigning group, said: “Across east Africa, people are facing the devastating impacts of a climate crisis they did not cause. From prolonged droughts to destructive floods, we are losing homes, livelihoods and access to food and water. Yet the money needed to respond to these impacts remains out of reach for many of the countries and communities most affected. It is fundamentally unjust that those least responsible are being left to carry the greatest costs.”

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

This summer more than 50,000 people died in Europe due to heatwaves, severe wildfire smoke shrouded swathes of North America and a glacier collapse in Nepal triggered a landslide that caused more than 1,500 deaths, while the El Niño weather event, described as “staggering” by scientists and still to reach its peak, is gathering strength.

However, while the pace of progress on cutting global emissions has been slow, the Iran war has spurred many countries to see fossil fuels as expensive and vulnerable to shocks, while renewables continue to plummet in price.

Simon Stiell, the UN’s climate chief, said before the talks: “Climate impacts hit every population and every economy harder each year. [But] almost every major economy, and many medium-sized and smaller economies, now see climate action as a central driver of economic growth, jobs, and affordable and secure energy.”

Cop host Turkey also wants more countries to switch to electric technologies, such as electric vehicles and heat pumps, as these are many times more efficient than their fossil fuel counterparts. A target for 35% of global final energy to come from electricity by 2035 will be on the table, but faces opposition from some.

Both presidency countries rely heavily on fossil fuels. Australia is one of the world’s leading exporters of coal and liquefied natural gas. The centre-left Labor government has targets to reach net zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050, and have more than 80% of its electricity generated by solar and wind by 2030, but is off track to meet them.

Labor also faces a political threat from the resurgent rightwing One Nation party, which dismisses climate science. One Nation and the Liberal and National party coalition want to scrap the net zero goal.

The UK energy secretary, Miatta Fahnbulleh, said: “This summer, we all felt it: climate change is here, in Britain, now. No country can tackle it alone, which is why Cop31 matters. The answer to high bills and climate change is the same – more clean, homegrown power, creating good jobs and warmer homes. Cop31 is about Britain’s national interest.”

#90. Kenyan doomsday cult leader given life sentence for murdering 191 people

Paul Mackenzie's followers believed he was "Jesus's mouthpiece", the judge said last month

A Kenyan court has sentenced the leader of a doomsday cult, Paul Mackenzie, to 200 years in prison for murdering 191 people, all but one of them children.

Alongside 28 others, Mackenzie was found guilty of the murders for instructing members of his cult to starve their children to death so they could "meet Jesus".

Justice Diana Mochache said Mackenzie bore the "primary criminal culpability" for ordering his followers to fast to death, in one of the world's deadliest episodes of cult activity.

"The sentence is appropriate and just, to send an unmistakable message that such atrocities warrant the maximum penalties allowed by law," the judge added.

The self-styled pastor was arrested in 2023 after the discovery of more than 400 bodies in shallow graves in Shakahola forest near the Kenyan coast. The sprawling investigation into the cases shocked Kenya and drew international attention.

The court sentenced Mackenzie to 100 years in prison for homicide and another 100 years for offences related to starvation.

The sentences within each category will run concurrently, but the two sets will run consecutively, bringing his total prison term to 200 years.

Mackenzie also faces separate manslaughter and terrorism charges, which he denies.

Prosecutors had sought the death penalty, arguing that the scale and circumstances of the killings warranted the harshest punishment.

However, Justice Mochache declined to impose the death penalty, despite ruling that the case met the threshold for its consideration.

"Nevertheless, the court declines the invitation to impose the death penalty on any of the victims," she said, adding that capital punishment would eliminate any prospect of rehabilitation and disengagement. The death sentence has not been carried out in Kenya since 1987.

Mackenzie was tried alongside 28 accomplices, including guards who enforced his fasting orders and gravediggers who helped conceal the victims' bodies in mass graves.

They received varying prison sentences of up to 160 years.

Justice Mochache described the killings as "cold and calculated", saying the perpetrators had shown no remorse and had even declared their willingness to fast to death themselves.

Before the hearing began on Friday morning, Francis Wanje, who lost eight relatives including two grandchildren in Shakahola forest, told the BBC's Newsday programme that he was "hoping for... a sentence which will match his [Mackenzie's] deeds in the forest".

Mackenzie and the co-accused had asked for leniency from the court.

Before their conviction on 19 October, they had all pleaded not guilty and repeatedly insisted that nobody was forced to die.

More than 400 bodies were dug up in Shakahola forest

Mackenzie's followers believed he was "Jesus's mouthpiece", Justice Mochache said as she delivered her eight-hour judgement last month.

Those who opposed Mackenzie's teachings were beaten to death.

One of his co-accused said he had told his followers that anyone who "resisted the directive to fast must be forced to go to heaven through kicks and blows".

Burials inside the forest where the cult was based were called "weddings" and those who died "were celebrated as having wedded with Jesus" the judge said.

The judge found that the deaths arose from an organised system, including religious teachings and directives that compelled followers to follow the deadly fasting.

Post-mortem results showed the cause of deaths to be starvation for most victims, while others died from suffocation, strangulation or head injuries.

The cause of death could not be determined for some, with their bodies having decomposed, while others died soon after their rescue as they were so emaciated.

The Shakahola deaths have shone a spotlight on the lack of oversight of rogue religious activities and how they could have carried on for so long.

The gravity of the case prompted President William Ruto to set up a commission of inquiry, although this was later declared unconstitutional.

Investigations by the state-funded Kenya National Commission on Human Rights (KNCHR) revealed that local administration and law enforcement officials had repeatedly turned a blind eye to the cult's activities.

"We expected at that time the police would take action immediately to go and rescue the other members there, but it didn't happen," said Wanje, who reported the case to the police in March 2023.

Wanje said a relative of his in the police force and a former cult participant had managed to save his grandson at the time. The child told the police what had happened and showed them the grave of his siblings.

"It was [supposed to be] his last day.. they [would have killed] my grandson."

The cult leader had previously been arrested and later released for lack of evidence.

Mackenzie established Good News International Ministries in 2003.

Over the next 16 years, the church grew in popularity, drawing thousands of followers in its branches across Kenya.

The church ran a television station called Times TV which would broadcast his sermons from his church in Malindi.

His End Days sermons included conspiracy theories that described education as "evil", healthcare as "against the doctrine" and digital identity cards as "the mark of the devil".

The main church shut down in 2019 and Mackenzie moved to Shakahola forest, encouraging his devotees to follow him.

While the conclusion of the murder cases may bring a sense of justice for the victims and their families, the convictions only account for 191 victims, while more than 400 bodies were exhumed from the forest.
